Originally published: October 7, 2009
Author: Carl Jung
Original title: Liber Novus ("The New Book")
Original language: German
Page count: 404

[8/21/2019 5:45 PM] hthazard
Our most recent advice from 2016, two years of trying to crack our copy of Liber Primus:

__**The path lies empty**__
-The current path that we were on in 2016, trying to solve __Cicada's Liber Primus__ for two years/no progress, is empty. We have been on this same path with no progress for __5 years now__?

__**Epiphany seeks the devoted**__
-This may imply that those who are devoted, *inherently look elsewhere*, meet epiphany

__**Liber Primus is the way**__
-__Directly contradicts__ their first statement about how our path lays empty, if *Cicada's* Liber Primus is the way. How about __Jung's Liber Primus __being the way? This would still be grammatically correct, and they wouldn't be contradicting themselves from the sentence prior.

__**{{Its}} words are the map**__
-A singular LP has words that are our map. Jung's Liber Primus is the map that has a path for us, ours in Cicada's LP has laid empty for 5 years.

__**{{Their}} meaning is the road**__
-Originally taken as the *'[The meaning of Cicada's LP's words] is the road'*, but it's grammatically viable to be *'[The meaning of Both LP's] is the road]* or even just *'[The meaning of Jung LP] is the road]*

__**{{Their}} numbers are the direction**__
-__Think about this.__
- It's grammatically incorrect to use '***[Their] numbers***' when referring to the aforementioned __singular Liber Primus__.
-The correct way would've been '***Its numbers are the direction***' like they previously used with '__***Its***__ ***words are the map***'.
-Because they stated '***Their numbers***', there are two possibilities:
-------A. '***Their numbers***', __plural__, intentionally referring to the **two** Liber Primus' numbers
-------B. '***Their numbers***' was a grammar mistake, and they should've used '***Its numbers***' while referring to a singular LP, like they did with '__***Its***__ ***words are the map***'

Cetus
Cetus () is a constellation. Its name refers to Cetus, a sea monster in Greek mythology, although it is often called 'the whale' today. Cetus is located in the region of the sky that contains other water-related constellations such as Aquarius, Pisces, and Eridanus.

[9/14/2019 11:38 AM] .mortlach
I think of it this way: 
1. We know the cipher text 
2. we 'know' the message  (well we guess, but there are only finite possibilities) 
3. For a chosen encryption method we can work out the key 
4. If we spot a pattern in the key that 'makes sense' then maybe we have solved the page 

This is how 56.jpg was solved, we spotted the prime sequence in the keys when trying lots of 2 and 3 rune phrases 

For this work, i'm seeing if the pattern in the key is "it is an English phrase," i can do quite well at it, probably too well, as I am generating too many phases. Therefore I'm asking is there a better method, that is fast and robust, to spot english phrases in a key that are worth keeping

F is the first (atbashed last) letter of the alphabet. Lining the added/subtracted keys up together after streaming/atbashing I got these 2 strings here:
`TH E  D  OE W  E  X  A  F  A  H ING EA T  G  S        paired`
`N  X CK  EO F  L  F  T  S  H  R  O  OE AE D  OE`
I theorize that since it is the first/last number, you're supposed to use these two keys together to build the plaintext, where you see an F in line with the other letter, you're to +/- 1 number to that letter to change it.

> pg 33/50
> ```text
> first word, two runes: ᛞᛇ
> position values: 23,12 => 2312
> base60 convert: (2*10*60)+(3*60)+(1*10)+(2)
> out value: 1392
> rev digits: 2931 (1033,3301 hint)
> split to pairs: 29,31
> since the length is halved during paring, duplicate result
> duped: 2931, 2931
> refering back to cuneiform/babylonin math format and the 1033,3301 hint swap pair order on one value
> key: 3129, 2931
> first word after applying stream cipher and then atbash
> plaintext: TH,E
> ```
> I have the entire section converted, working on swapping pairs. also, I am extracting position values of 0 (F) and then inserting them in their original positions after conversion because there is no zero in cuneiform.
> 
> also, in an attempt to ensure this process would generate a key of appropriate length, I have dumped some metric rom the process
> ```text
> pos values:      1680
> skips:           40
> converted:       820
> expand (dupe):   1640
> after inject 0:  1680
> key length:      1680
> 
> key length === pos values length
> ```

[11/7/2020 8:04 PM] 7he5haman
Perhaps the Mod 60 and "3301 // 1033" hints from the cuneiform could be applied to the GP?

I’m thinking along the lines of the ‘Gematria Primus 2014', per the wiki.

Specifically, applying Mod 60 to the 29 GP values yields a prime in all but two cases (61 = 1 Mod 60 and 109 = 49 Mod 60; 1 and 49 are squares, at least!) - *this might explain why Base 60 pops up a lot?!*

Applying Mod 60 to the GP values and then matching those which are identical would yield the following:

F (2)
U (3) 
TH (5)
O (7) <---> E (67)
R (11) <---> M (71)
C/K (13) <---> L (73)
G (17) 
W (19) <---> NG/ING (79)
H (23) <---> OE (83)
N (29) <---> D (89)
I (31)
J (37) <---> A (97)
EO (41)  <---> AE (101)
P (43) <---> Y (103)
X (47) <---> IA/IO (107)
B (61) or (1)
S/Z (53)
T (59)
EA (109) or (49)

G (17) ---> X (47) ---> IA/IO (107) ---> G (17)

H (23) ---> S/Z (53) ---> OE (83) ---> H (23)
N (29) --->  T (59) ---> D (89) ---> N (29)
I (31) <---> B (61)


Without the numbers:

[1/10/2021 10:39 AM] .mortlach
of course,
we can map runes to numbers in different ways. For example,  the 2 gematria orders:
F = 0, U = 1, TH = 2 ...  EA = 28
F = 28, U= 27, TH = 26 .... EA = 0
we can shift those orders by +- 29 
F = 1, U = 2, TH = 3 ...  EA = 27 etc.
That gives us 58 ways of assigning an index to a rune 
therefore a base 58 number could be used to define how to encode as a rune as a number.
we also have a 59th way of encoding a rune as a number, their rune prime value:
F = 2 U = 3 TH = 5 etc
i think this this means we can use base 59 numbers to encode runes
of course this is somewhat arbitrary, and if it weren't for the clear base 60 hints i doubt i would be mentioning it


[1/10/2021 10:46 AM] .mortlach
it is mixing two things together and 
just an idea to integrate base 59 numbers with gematria encoding


[1/10/2021 10:47 AM] .mortlach
i think the prime numbers are assigned by the puzzle makers,
the index derived from the "order" is somewhat arbitrary, 
and so every solution has other equivalent solutions using a different gematria rotation/order

I

EO <---> AE

[4/24/2021 11:29 PM] inky2492
@sikas A letter can be any other letter.  What you describe works for things like atbash, where each letter decodes to one particular letter every time.  In the rune atbash code used on some of the decoded pages, F is always EA, U is always IA, and so on, right enough.


[4/24/2021 11:33 PM] inky2492
But in the code used on the "An End" page, the runes are shifted by the sequence of prime numbers minus 1.  They shifted the first rune on the page by 1 (the first prime number, 2, minus 1), the second rune by 2 (the second prime number, 3, minus 1), the third rune by 4 (the third prime number, 5, minus 1), the fourth rune by 6 (the fourth prime number, 7, minus 1), and so on.


[4/24/2021 11:33 PM] inky2492
So any rune could become any rune, depending where it was on the page.


[4/24/2021 11:34 PM] inky2492
It was a good idea, but I don't think it'll work.

[4/28/2021 4:17 PM] avaster5567
```
OEIS oeisdb = OEIS();

for (const auto &v : oeisdb.get_sequence(1))
{
    std::cout << v << " ";
}
std::cout << std::endl;

oeisdb.pre_sequence_modification = [](uint64_t num){return num + 5;};

for (const auto &v : oeisdb.get_sequence(1))
{
    std::cout << v << " ";
}
std::cout << std::endl;

[10/6/2021 10:08 PM] kindr3d0595
On page 33 - We see Cuneiform that translates to: 10 7 10 3 & 50 5 1
if you take 10 +7, combine it to 17 and multiply it by 60 (for it's a sexagesimal system) and finish by adding 13, you get 1103
likewise with the second set, it's end result is 3301

I think this, while not the solution, is possibly another hint for the rest of the page. 

When looking up Sexagesimal systems, you can find that "In Hellenistic Greek astronomical texts, such as the writings of Ptolemy, sexagesimal numbers were written using Greek alphabetic numerals, with each sexagesimal digit being treated as a distinct number."
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexagesimal

Greeks had a system similar to Gematria, called Isopsephy, and according to wikipedia: "An early reference to isopsephy, albeit of more-than-usual sophistication (employing multiplication rather than addition), is from the mathematician Apollonius of Perga, writing in the 3rd century BC. He asks: "Given the verse: ΑΡΤΕΜΙΔΟΣ ΚΛΕΙΤΕ ΚΡΑΤΟΣ ΕΞΟΧΟΝ ΕΝΝΕΑ ΚΟΥΡΑΙ ('Nine maidens, praise the glorious power of Artemis'), what does the product of all its elements equal?"[4] " 
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_numerals#Hellenistic_zero

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isopsephy

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexagesimal
Sexagesimal
Sexagesimal, also known as base 60 or sexagenary, is a numeral system with sixty as its base. It originated with the ancient Sumerians in the 3rd millennium BC, was passed down to the ancient Babylonians, and is still used—in a modified form—for measuring time, angles, and geographic coordinates.
The number 60, a superior highly composite number...

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isopsephy
Isopsephy
Isopsephy (; ἴσος isos meaning "equal" and ψῆφος psephos meaning "pebble") or isopsephism is the practice of adding up the number values of the letters in a word to form a single number. The total number is then used as a metaphorical bridge to other words evaluating the equal number, which satisfies isos or "equal" in the term. The early Greeks...

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_numerals
Greek numerals
Greek numerals, also known as Ionic, Ionian, Milesian, or Alexandrian numerals, are a system of writing numbers using the letters of the Greek alphabet. In modern Greece, they are still used for ordinal numbers and in contexts similar to those in which Roman numerals are still used in the Western world. For ordinary cardinal numbers, however, mo...


[10/6/2021 10:09 PM] kindr3d0595
Apollonius of Perga shares an interesting link with the cuneiform. Perga sounds similar to Persia, which is related to the cuneiform found at the bottom of the page. Additionally, the three dots, could be representative of the millenium of the use of cuneiform, as well as the century where Isopsephy was used. A quote from wikipedia on Apollonius; "Eutocius appears to associate Perga with the Ptolemaic dynasty of Egypt..." "...Someone designated “of Perga” might well be expected to have lived and worked there."
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollonius_of_Perga

and according to the same page:
A letter by the Greek mathematician and astronomer Hypsicles was originally part of the supplement taken from Euclid's Book XIV, part of the thirteen books of Euclid's Elements.[6] 

13 dots = Euclid's 13 books.

Within Euclid's 13th book, there is a paragraph that points to a set of 5 Postulates contained within Euclid's first book. This corresponds perfectly to the rest of the section, which contains 5 red "points" (p36-38 of LP) Also, interestingly enough, I see quite a bit of similarity in the design of both the Liber Primus and the Elements marginalia.
https://archive.org/details/thirteenbookseu03heibgoog/page/n453/mode/2up?view=theater&q=Antiphon
https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Euclid%27s_Elements%2C_1482.jpg/1024px-Euclid%27s_Elements%2C_1482.jpg
http://aleph0.clarku.edu/~djoyce/java/elements/bookI/bookI.html#posts


I'm not really sure where to take it from here, but these seem like it's definitely in the right direction.

[11/7/2021 8:36 PM] _complexsystems7110
^ Agree. It's pretty clear that these numbers are important. Notably, 59 is also fully reptend prime. 

Also worth noting that Babylonians did not really have a base60 system. Because of their form of place-value notation and because they didn't have the number zero, they could only go up to 59 before the representational cycle had to be repeated.

[1/3/2022 4:45 PM] fib11235
3301/1033 = a repeating decimal with a period of 1032
1033/3301 = a repeating decimal with a period of 3300

{Reactions}
pog (2)

[1/3/2022 5:04 PM] codewarrior0
The repeating part is divisible by 9, according to Wikipedia:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeating_decimal

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeating_decimal
Repeating decimal
A repeating decimal or recurring decimal is decimal representation of a number whose digits are periodic (repeating its values at regular intervals) and the infinitely repeated portion is not zero. It can be shown that a number is rational if and only if its decimal representation is repeating or terminating (i.e. all except finitely many digits...

[2/17/2022 4:25 PM] kylestanley
Also, I think the point mrgermanpiano was getting at earlier, is that you cannot have an n×n matrix(square matrix) with 29 entrues. You literally can't have any m×n matrix where m and n are greater than 1 that has a prime number of entries

{Reactions}
T_ (2)

[2/17/2022 8:23 PM] codewarrior0
This sounds similar to the Hill cipher. For that, your matrices don't contain every letter of the alphabet. They just represent the encryption and decryption keys. This kind of cipher happens to be easier to implement when the size of your alphabet is a prime number.


[2/17/2022 8:25 PM] codewarrior0
Conversely, some types of vigenere-family ciphers are easier to *attack* when the alphabet size is prime, because the attacker doesn't have to be on guard for something called the "even decimation".


[2/17/2022 9:42 PM] woodford666
Devil’s advocate, the same is true for the Latin alphabet and the polybius square. You either drop a letter (eg Q) or two letters take up the same square (eg I/J). Both decisions based on the the fact the Q and J have very low frequency in English text.

You can run a similar flow with GP. Both ae and oe are super low frequency in English, and J of course. You _could_ find your way to a 5x5 square.

There’s some precedent for that character “merging” in GP already: s/z, ng/ing, ia/io.

[7/23/2022 12:10 PM] nostalgico
“These manners of hers were soon known to the Moor, who would by no means neglect such an opportunity, and because her steward did not pay sufficient attention to her, she was easily blinded with great promises, so that she did not keep good confidence with her King, but privately submitted herself entirely to the disposal of the Moor. Hereupon the Moor made haste, and having (by her consent) got her into his hands, he gave her good words until all her kingdom had subjected itself to him. After which, in the third scene of this act, he caused her to be led forth, and first to be stripped stark naked, and then to be bound to a post upon a scurvy wooden scaffold, and well scourged, and at last sentenced to death. This was so woeful a spectacle, that it made the eyes of many run over. Hereupon like this, naked as she was, she was cast into prison, there to await her death, which was to be procured by poison, which actually did not kill her, but made her leprous all over. Thus this act was for the most part lamentable.  

Between acts, they brought forth *Nebuchadnezzar’s image*, which was adorned with all manner of arms, on the head, breast, belly, legs and feet, and the like, of which more shall be said in the future explanation.
In the fifth act the young King was told of all that had passed between the Moor and his future spouse; he first interceded with his father for her, entreating that she might not be left in that condition; which his father having agreed to, ambassadors were despatched to comfort her in her sickness and captivity, but yet also to make her see her inconsiderateness. But she still would not receive them, but consented to be the Moor’s concubine, which was also done, and the young King was acquainted with it.”

[4/14/2023 1:37 AM] bachtothelab
Been doing a lot of reading into how the babylonians performed operations in cuneiform. Multiplying by reciprocals meant they didn't have to deal with fractions and everything reduced down to whole numbers. They used reciprocal tables to do so, so I'm gonna try some things with that. 

I feel like using Mod 59 makes sense, as 0-58 can represent 1-59 (they used 1 again for 60) as they didn't use zero.


[4/14/2023 1:43 AM] bachtothelab
I wonder why they included 1033 over 3301 in this section instead of next to each other? Maybe hinting at division, atbash, mirroring, etc?  Bbyln cuneiform just used spacing between numbers as demarcation that they are separate. Their operations progressed from top to bottom in showing steps on how to perform computations. Could that be hinting at getting 3301 from 1033? (Or the shifted position equivalents, as by what power of 60 to multiply by isn't clear off the bat and requires assumption).

The cuneiform appearing at the bottom of 33 and the top of the rest makes me think whatever cipher it uses starts at the 2nd red header section on the same page. They had enough room at the top, otherwise it doesn't make sense why to switch that.

{Reactions}
👍

[4/14/2023 1:48 AM] bachtothelab
Apparently there are hundreds of thousands of these clay tablets, and more than half of them have not been translated. You can translate them yourself if you know the conventions. What are the chances 3301 is referencing a specific tablet that they find significant? (Like the one outlining pythagorean triples 1000 years before pythagoras) you can find these in museums all over the world, so I wouldn't find that too far fetched. Probably not helpful to find though unless it was a tablet that specifically talked about primes.

Something interesting though is that you get 1033 from the representation of two prime numbers in cuneiform. Maybe we're supposed to see what other primes we can get too?

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexagesimal
Sexagesimal
Sexagesimal, also known as base 60 or sexagenary, is a numeral system with sixty as its base. It originated with the ancient Sumerians in the 3rd millennium BC, was passed down to the ancient Babylonians, and is still used—in a modified form—for measuring time, angles, and geographic coordinates.
The number 60, a superior highly composite number...


[4/14/2023 11:48 AM] .sltn
"Because there was no symbol for zero it is not always immediately obvious how a number should be interpreted, and its true value must sometimes have been determined by its context. For example, the symbols for 1 and 60 are identical.[5][6] Later Babylonian texts used a placeholder (Babylonian digit 0.svg) to represent zero, but only in the medial positions, and not on the right-hand side of the number, as in numbers like 13200.[6]"

[4/19/2023 4:25 PM] bachtothelab
Operations proceeded from top to bottom, the leftmost number of any listed is the lowest position, so ... 60^2, 60^1, 60^0, 60^-1, 60^-2 ...

The problem is you have to use context to tell what place they are using. Is it 60s and 1s place for 2 cuneiform numbers? 1s and 1/60s? You technically have to account for all possibilities when assessing their numerical value. 

So 17 and 13 (like in our pages) could be 17×60^1 + 13×60^0 = 1020+13 = 1033.

OR it could technically be moved to the right or left, e.g. 17×60^2 + 13×60^1, or 17×60^0 + 13×60^-1, respectively, are both valid (as are further shifts in either directions)


[4/19/2023 4:25 PM] bachtothelab
All you know for sure is that the 17's position means it is 60× whatever is in the 13's position.


[4/19/2023 4:54 PM] lin_sivvi

[9/4/2023 12:07 AM] heck80085
I found something interesting. If we wish to arrive at "THE FIVE", we take the original GP Value minus the Message GP Value and add primes in sequence, skipping every 3rd prime, we arrive at NEW PRIMES that can be connected to the GP.

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/613322517360410624/1148106960097513482/image.png?ex=6a7013ee&is=6a6ec26e&hm=07105fd3c5e6baa6db6b2e61fd9a2c6473c880df4f1aba57cf9e143f75d20d0e&

{Reactions}
👀

[9/4/2023 12:51 AM] heck80085
It's repeating if I do the following:


[9/4/2023 12:54 AM] heck80085
ORIGINAL GP + next prime in sequence, every 3rd skipped. Then I take the original key and minus it to that value

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/613322517360410624/1148118788861087774/image.png?ex=6a6f7632&is=6a6e24b2&hm=285cfbd7cda8a92c8bb46afff6fd712caf256c3e776564ac98af30ef88ebde23&

Guys

[9/4/2023 12:57 AM] heck80085
CIPHER = Original GP Value + Primes in sequence (every 3rd skipped)
KEY = CIPHER - MSG (the GP values my guess of "THE FIVE"), then repeat minus-ing those 5 values

MSG = CIPHER - KEY

Cuneiform
Cuneiform is a logo-syllabic writing system that was used to write several languages of the Ancient Near East. The script was in active use from the early Bronze Age until the beginning of the Common Era. Cuneiform scripts are marked by and named for the characteristic wedge-shaped impressions (Latin: cuneus) which form their signs. Cuneiform is...

[8/30/2025 10:26 AM] lhjvn
```python
def inverse_totient(k):
    if k < 1:
        return []

    result = []
    for n in range(2, 2 * k + 1):
        if phi(n) == k:
            result.append(n)
    return result

def phi(n):
    result = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= n:
        if n % p == 0:
            while n % p == 0:
                n //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1
    if n > 1:
        result -= result // n
    return result
print(inverse_totient(55*60))
print(inverse_totient(17*60))
```

[5/28/2026 12:16 PM] ivi17
As there is this cuneiform marginalia, where 1033 includes the number 17: 17×60+13 = 1033 (not to mention the importance of this number in the cicada life cycle), I think it's interesting the presence of 17 (degrees) in the Babylonian calendar (>cuneiform>Sumer, etc. Their MUL.APIN star compendium was written with cuneiform on clay tablets). 
All 3 celestial paths are defined by this number 17:
The Path of Ea: The southern hemisphere (below 17° South).
The Path of Anu: The celestial equator (between 17° North and South).
The Path of Enlil: The northern hemisphere (above 17° North).
Also: "The path of Anu is considered as a belt around the celestial equator with a width of roughly ±17° that is divided in twelve equal parts of 30° length, representing ideal months." 
Other interesting link, this time with the 3 dots, visually dividing p. 39: The first Babyloninan star lists compendium was called "Three Stars Each" (=to each of the 12 babylonian months 3 specific stars - or constellations -  were asigned). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_star_catalogues#Three_Stars_Each

{Embed}
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_star_catalogues
Babylonian star catalogues
Babylonian astronomy collated earlier observations and divinations into sets of Babylonian star catalogues, during and after the Kassite rule over Babylonia. These star catalogues, written in cuneiform script, contained lists of constellations, individual stars, and planets. The constellations were probably collected from various other sources. ...
https://images-ext-1.discordapp.net/external/U6190w7_yuGi5qpYx_O0dfA6rN5aNT33cszBTEp-M9U/https/upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/-200_Sternenliste_aus_Uruk_star_list_anagoria.JPG/1280px--200_Sternenliste_aus_Uruk_star_list_anagoria.JPG

[6/14/2026 3:49 PM] ivi17
Another thing about the first two dots, p.39. If you check the shades of black and grey of their pixels, you'll see that they're perfectly mirrored. The first two dots aren't just equal by dimensions; they aren't copies of each other. They're absolute **mirrors ** one to another, in terms of exact rgb values for each pixel.  
For example the 1st pixel row of the first dot RGB: 203, 194, 107, 96, 25, 20, 23, 22, 97, 106, 202, 206 is repeated in reverse on 1st row of the second dot. 
That is surely a consequence of the specific technique for data conversion cicada used combined with the exact position of the dots on the page and the distances between them, but it's a matter of choice.
